- **Tags**: `entanglement`  `noise`  `mixed-state entanglement`  `entanglement witnesses`  `partial transpose moments`

### TL;DR

The paper introduces efficiently measurable entanglement witnesses from partial transpose and realignment moments, yielding bounds on entanglement monotones, dimensionality, and many-body entanglement properties.

### Motivation

Quantifying entanglement in mixed quantum many-body systems is notoriously difficult, especially with limited measurement capabilities.

### Method

It constructs families of quantitative entanglement witnesses based on partial transpose and realignment moments that can be measured via SWAP tests or Bell measurement variants.

### Result

The witnesses provide rigorous bounds on entanglement monotones and dimensionality, enable entropy-bounded entanglement tests, Schmidt-rank certification, noise-robust circuit-depth certification, and entanglement phase-diagram characterization, and imply limits on pseudoentanglement and pseudorandom density matrices.

### Conclusion

This work opens new avenues for quantifying entanglement in large noisy quantum systems and connects entanglement quantification to quantum cryptography and many-body physics.

*Keywords: mixed-state entanglement, entanglement witnesses, partial transpose moments, realignment moments, SWAP tests*

### TL;DR

The paper constructs a number-conserving many-body state that exhibits a Rényi-index-dependent transition in the leading scaling of entanglement entropy, generalizing prior results to interacting fermions and providing a symmetry-aware lower bound from charge-resolved Rényi entropies.

### Motivation

The von Neumann entropy is experimentally and numerically costly, so the second Rényi entropy is often used as a proxy, but its scaling can differ from von Neumann scaling in constrained spin models. The work aims to generalize this anomalous behavior to interacting fermions and offer an experimentally accessible diagnostic.

### Method

The authors explicitly build a number-conserving many-body state and analyze the subsystem-size scaling of its von Neumann and Rényi entropies for different Rényi indices. They derive a symmetry-aware lower bound on the von Neumann entropy using charge-resolved Rényi entropies.

### Result

They demonstrate a Rényi-index-dependent change in the leading entanglement scaling, including transitions between power-law and logarithmic behavior, in an interacting fermion setting. The charge-resolved bound can detect such anomalous scaling from quantities accessible in two-copy measurement protocols.

### Conclusion

Rényi entropies do not always track von Neumann entanglement scaling, so care is needed when using them as proxies. Charge-resolved Rényi entropies provide a practical protocol for diagnosing anomalous entanglement scaling in experiments and simulations.

*Keywords: entanglement entropy, Rényi entropy, entanglement scaling, interacting fermions, charge-resolved entropy*

- **Tags**: `belief-propagation`  `quantum LDPC codes`  `belief-propagation decoding`  `Tanner graph girth`  `orthogonality constraints`

### TL;DR

A novel construction of quantum LDPC codes that breaks the orthogonality barrier by using controlled-commutativity permutation matrices, achieving large girth and good BP decoding performance.

### Motivation

Quantum LDPC codes require orthogonality between parity-check matrices, which typically reduces Tanner graph girth and structurally bounds minimum distance, limiting BP decoding performance.

### Method

The author employs permutation matrices with controlled commutativity and restricts orthogonality constraints to only the active part of the construction while preserving regular check-matrix structures.

### Result

A girth-8, (3,12)-regular quantum LDPC code is constructed, and under BP decoding with low-complexity post-processing it achieves a frame error rate as low as 10^-5 on the depolarizing channel at error probability 0.1.

### Conclusion

The approach circumvents conventional structural distance limitations, enabling quantum LDPC codes with large girth and no latent low-weight logical operators, thus advancing practical quantum error correction.

*Keywords: quantum LDPC codes, belief-propagation decoding, Tanner graph girth, orthogonality constraints, quantum error correction*

Traditional quantum measurement formalism assumes that the observer stores only classical information, so it cannot describe how an agent with quantum memory would observe a quantum system. This raises the fundamental question of how information gain and disturbance trade off for quantum agents.

### Result

For appropriate sensory interactions, a quantum agent can learn more about the system than any set of classical measurements, but this advantage comes at the cost of additional measurement disturbance. The tradeoff is experimentally characterized.

### Conclusion

The work broadens measurement theory to quantum agents with quantum memory, revealing a novel information-disturbance tradeoff and quantifying it through erasure channel capacity.

Measurement incompatibility is a fundamental quantum resource, but its quantitative role in Bayesian multiparameter estimation was not fully understood. The authors aim to provide a comprehensive framework and practical bounds for assessing ultimate precision limits.

### Method

They derive upper bounds using the pretty good measurement from hypothesis testing and evaluate the Nagaoka-Hayashi lower bound. Applications to discrete phase imaging, phase and dephasing estimation, and qubit sensing illustrate the approach, with an open-source package for all bounds.

### Result

They prove that, as in many-copy local estimation, incompatibility can at most double the minimum loss relative to the idealised scenario with jointly implementable individually optimal measurements. This implies the idealised benchmark is often sufficient and computationally efficient.

### Motivation

Neutral-atom tweezer arrays require light shifts on ground and excited states to be equalized so that optical addressing and readout are free of differential shift, motivating precise magic-wavelength measurements for fermionic potassium.

### Result

The measured magic wavelength of 1227.54 nm agrees with relativistic all-order calculations and enables shift-free optical addressing of 40K in tweezer arrays.

### Conclusion

### TL;DR

This paper studies the algebraic structure of the Quantum Max d-Cut problem for qudits, develops a semidefinite programming hierarchy for its solution, and derives exact results for complete bipartite graphs using representation theory.

### Motivation

Quantum Max Cut is a prominent 2-local Hamiltonian problem in computational complexity, and its higher-dimensional qudit generalization is less understood, motivating algebraic and computational tools for solving it.

### Method

The authors identify the algebra generated by qudit swap operators as a quotient of a free algebra with symmetric group relations and an extra relation, then construct a tailored noncommutative polynomial optimization hierarchy of semidefinite programs and use symmetric group representation theory.

### Result

The hierarchy converges to the solution of d-QMC; exact solutions are obtained for a large class of complete bipartite graphs; and the star graph spectrum distinguishes isotypic components, with low-degree relations enabling efficient component-wise computation.

### Conclusion

The work provides a systematic algebraic and optimization framework for Quantum Max d-Cut, yielding both algorithmic convergence and representation-theoretic exact solutions for structured graphs.

- **Tags**: `t-depth`  `quantum advantage`  `shallow circuits`  `sampling`  `circuit lower bounds`

### TL;DR

The paper unconditionally proves that constant-depth quantum circuits can sample from distributions that cannot be approximated by constant-depth bounded fan-in classical circuits, even with random inputs.

### Motivation

Bravyi, Gosset, and Koenig previously showed a search-problem separation and asked whether an input-independent sampling task could achieve a similar unconditional quantum advantage. This work answers that question affirmatively.

### Method

The authors introduce a distribution and construct a constant-depth uniform quantum circuit family that samples from a distribution close in total variation distance. They then prove a lower bound on the depth of any classical bounded fan-in circuit that takes i.i.d. Bernoulli random inputs and tries to approximate the same distribution.

### Result

They obtain an unconditional separation between constant-depth quantum circuits and constant-depth bounded fan-in classical circuits for sampling up to additive error. They also prove a similar separation between quantum circuits with advice and classical circuits with bounded fan-in and fan-out and access to an unbounded number of random inputs.

### Conclusion

This establishes a new unconditional quantum advantage for shallow sampling tasks, extending previous separations from search problems to input-independent sampling and strengthening the case for quantum computational advantages in low-depth regimes.

*Keywords: quantum advantage, shallow circuits, sampling, circuit lower bounds, constant-depth circuits*

### TL;DR

The paper develops two protocols using NV-center spin qubits—dynamical-decoupling spin-echo sequences and two-qubit coincidence measurements—to extract higher-order (non-Gaussian) magnetic noise cumulants with nanometer spatial resolution.

### Motivation

NV-center magnetometry has so far been limited to Gaussian noise processes, equivalent to linear response, leaving higher-order correlations and collective-mode spectra in quantum materials largely inaccessible.

### Method

One protocol uses a single spin qubit with tailored dynamical decoupling sequences to isolate non-Markovian and non-Gaussian spin-echo noise; a second uses two-qubit coincidence measurements to probe spatially non-local noise cumulants, demonstrated on a bath of non-interacting two-level systems and on spatially correlated fluctuations near a second-order Ising transition.

### Result

The protocols can unambiguously measure higher-order magnetic noise cumulants locally and in a spatially resolved manner, and track how fluctuation dynamics converge to the central limit theorem as a function of effective bath size.

### Conclusion

The work extends NV-based noise spectroscopy beyond the Gaussian regime, offering a tool to characterize non-linear and spatially correlated fluctuations in many-body quantum materials, with further extensions discussed.

*Keywords: NV-center spin qubits, non-Gaussian noise spectroscopy, dynamical decoupling, higher-order cumulants, quantum magnetometry*

### Method

### TL;DR

This Perspective quantifies the quantum resources needed to break elliptic-curve cryptography (as used in cryptocurrencies) and argues that the threat is closer and cheaper than commonly assumed, urging migration to post-quantum security.

### Motivation

Elliptic-curve cryptography underpins most cryptocurrencies and much of the public-key infrastructure, and its security rests on the presumed hardness of the discrete logarithm problem, which Shor's algorithm solves on a sufficiently large fault-tolerant quantum computer.

### Method

The authors produce detailed resource estimates (qubit counts, gate counts, T-depth, runtime) for running Shor's algorithm to solve the elliptic-curve discrete logarithm problem at the relevant key sizes, comparing architectural variants and error-correction overheads.

### Result

The estimates indicate that cryptographically relevant attacks against elliptic-curve keys require substantially fewer physical qubits and less time than prior analyses suggested, making the quantum threat more imminent than the community assumed.

### Conclusion

The authors conclude that timely migration to post-quantum cryptographic schemes is urgent, framing the paper as both a threat assessment and a roadmap for cryptocurrency and protocol stakeholders.

### Motivation

Near a quantum critical point, low-temperature thermodynamics is expected to follow universal scaling laws, but direct measurements of universal magnetocaloric scaling functions have been lacking.

### Method

Adiabatic demagnetization measurements and nuclear magnetic resonance were used on a spin-1/2 antiferromagnetic Heisenberg chain compound to probe the magnetocaloric effect across the quantum critical regime.

### Result

### Method

### Result

### TL;DR

Coherent electron transmission across twin boundaries in Cu(111) films is observed via scanning tunneling spectroscopy, showing high transmission and phase coherence due to structural symmetry and identical Fermi surfaces.

### Motivation

Copper thin films are limited by electron scattering at atomic-scale defects like twin boundaries; understanding whether these defects preserve coherence could enable low-dissipation nanoscale electronics.

### Method

Scanning tunneling spectroscopy measurements on grain-boundary-free Cu(111) films grown by atomic sputtering epitaxy to probe quantum interference and transmission across twin boundaries and atomic step edges.

### Result

Pronounced quantum interference indicates non-dephasing elastic scattering across twin boundaries, with unexpectedly high transmission probabilities and preserved phase coherence due to the structural symmetry and identical Fermi surface on either side.

### Conclusion

Cu(111) can support highly transparent, coherent electron flow despite atomic discontinuities, without invoking topological protection, establishing a framework for using conventional metals in coherent, low-dissipation nanoscale electronic devices.

*Keywords: twin boundaries, Cu(111), scanning tunneling spectroscopy, coherent electron transport, quantum interference*
